# Syrian civil war

For the ongoing clashes since the [fall of the Assad regime](78548066.md), see [Aftermath of the Syrian civil war](81373641.md).

The **Syrian civil war** was an [armed conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_conflict) that began with the [Syrian revolution](39750126.md) in March 2011, when popular discontent with the [Ba'athist regime](57134800.md) ruled by [Bashar al-Assad](364813.md) triggered large-scale protests and [pro-democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro-democracy) rallies across [Syria](7515849.md), as part of the wider [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring). The Assad regime responded to the protests with lethal force, which led to a series of defections, the emergence of armed opposition groups, and the civilian uprising descending into a [civil war](7085.md). The war lasted almost 14 years and culminated in the [fall of the Assad regime](78548066.md) in December 2024. Many sources regard this as the end of the civil war[^cite_note-end_of_war-4] even though clashes have [continued](81373641.md) into 2026.&#x20;

The [Syrian opposition to Bashar al-Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_opposition_\(2011%E2%80%932024\)) began as [an insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Insurgency), forming groups such as the [Free Syrian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Syrian_Army) (FSA). Anti-Assad forces received arms and training from [Qatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qatari_involvement_in_the_Syrian_civil_war), [Turkey](11125639.md), a [United States-led program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timber_Sycamore), and others. Pro-Assad forces received financial and military support from [Iran](14653.md), [Russia](25391.md), and [Hezbollah](13919.md): Iran [launched a military intervention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_intervention_in_the_Syrian_civil_war) in support of the Syrian government in 2013, and [Russia followed in 2015](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_intervention_in_the_Syrian_civil_war). By this time, rebels had established the [Syrian Interim Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Interim_Government) after capturing the regional capitals of [Raqqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Raqqa_\(2013\)) in 2013 and [Idlib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Idlib_\(2015\)) in 2015. [Use of chemical weapons](39668667.md) during the war, predominantly by Syrian government forces, was the deadliest since the [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War). The [Ghouta sarin attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghouta_chemical_attack) was followed by unsuccessful international attempts to [eliminate Syria's chemical weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Destruction_of_Syria%27s_chemical_weapons) through military action or diplomacy.&#x20;

In 2014, the [Islamic State](9087364.md) (IS) seized control over [Eastern Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Eastern_Syria_offensive) and [Western Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Iraq_offensive_\(June_2014\)), prompting a [United States](3434750.md)-led [coalition](44618350.md) to launch an aerial [bombing campaign against the IS](43517689.md), while providing [ground support](43683196.md) and supplies to the [Syrian Democratic Forces](48205248.md) (SDF), a Kurdish-dominated coalition led by the [People's Defense Units](36497032.md) (YPG). In 2016, [Turkey](11125639.md) launched [an invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_involvement_in_the_Syrian_civil_war) of [northern Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_occupation_of_northern_Syria) in response to the creation of the Kurdish-led [Autonomous Administration of North and East Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Administration_of_North_and_East_Syria) (Rojava), while also establishing the [Syrian National Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_National_Army) (SNA) to help it [fight ISIS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Euphrates_Shield) and [pro-Assad forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Spring_Shield).&#x20;

The 2016 victory of the [Syrian Arab Army](78567599.md) (SAA) and other pro-Assad forces in the four-year [Battle of Aleppo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Aleppo_\(2012%E2%80%932016\)) marked the recapture of what had been Syria's largest city before the war. In [Idlib Governorate](7445043.md), the [Hay'at Tahrir al-Sham](53015755.md) (HTS) militia formed the [Syrian Salvation Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Salvation_Government), a technocratic, [Islamist](15012.md) administration that governed the region from 2017 until 2024. Meanwhile, IS was territorially defeated in the [Syrian Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Desert_campaign_\(May%E2%80%93July_2017\)), [Central Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Syria_campaign), [Raqqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raqqa_campaign_\(2016%E2%80%932017\)), and [Deir ez-Zor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deir_ez-Zor_campaign_\(2017%E2%80%932019\)) campaigns. In December 2019, the SAA and its allies launched an offensive on Idlib province, [which resulted in a ceasefire lasting from 2020 until November 2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwestern_Syria_offensive_\(2019%E2%80%932020\)). During this period, there were [regular clashes between pro-Assad forces and HTS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwestern_Syria_clashes_\(December_2022_%E2%80%93_November_2024\)).&#x20;

HTS launched a [major offensive](78460726.md) in November 2024, joined by the SNA. [Aleppo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Aleppo_\(2024\)) fell in three days as the SAA began to collapse, giving momentum to revolutionaries across the country. HTS soon captured [Hama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Hama_offensive) and began to advance south towards [Homs](77533.md). [Southern rebels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Front_\(Syria\)) and the [Syrian Free Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Free_Army) launched [their own offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Syria_offensive_\(2024\)), capturing [Daraa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daraa), [Suwayda](2590570.md), and [Palmyra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyra_offensive_\(2024\)). On 8 December, [Bashar al-Assad](364813.md) fled to [Moscow](19004.md) as [Homs fell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Homs_offensive) to HTS while southern rebels entered [Damascus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Damascus_\(2024\)). [Assad's prime minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Ghazi_al-Jalali) remained in Damascus and transferred power to [a provisional government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_caretaker_government). [Israel](9282173.md) launched an [invasion](78549398.md) of Syria's [Quneitra Governorate](7445647.md) (including the [UN buffer zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Disengagement_Observer_Force_Zone)) from its [58-year occupation](60364960.md) of the Syrian [Golan Heights](7515865.md).&#x20;

At the [Syrian Revolution Victory Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Revolution_Victory_Conference) held at the [Presidential Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_Palace,_Damascus) in [Damascus](8914.md) in January 2025, the new government announced the dissolution of several armed militias and their integration into the [Syrian Ministry of Defense](2774942.md), as well as the appointment of former HTS leader [Ahmed al-Sharaa](44007699.md) as [president of Syria](516106.md). Later that year, a [Druze insurgency](81009262.md) formed in the southern [Suweida Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suweida_Governorate) following clashes with the government and alleged sectarian violence.&#x20;

## Overview

The Syrian civil war was fought by several factions from 2011 to December 2024. The [Syrian Arab Armed Forces](78567599.md), alongside its domestic and foreign allies, represented the [Syrian Arab Republic](7515849.md#Ba'athist_Syria) ruled by Bashar al-Assad's [Ba'athist government](69538970.md). As the civil war continued, alternative governments rose in [opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_opposition_\(2011%E2%80%932024\)) to Assad's rule, such as the [Syrian Interim Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Interim_Government) (SIG), a [big-tent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_tent) alliance of pro-democratic, [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_nationalism) opposition groups whose armed forces consisted of the [Syrian National Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_National_Army) (SNA) and allied [Free Syrian militias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Syrian_militias). Another anti-Assad faction was the [Syrian Salvation Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Salvation_Government) (SSG), whose armed wing was represented by a [coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Operations_Command) of nationalist and [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Syria) [Islamist](15012.md) militias led by [Hay'at Tahrir al-Sham](53015755.md) (HTS). Independent of the SIG and SSG was the [Autonomous Administration of North and East Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Administration_of_North_and_East_Syria) (AANES), also known as Rojava, whose military force is the [Syrian Democratic Forces](48205248.md) (SDF), a multi-ethnic, Arab-majority force led by the [Kurdish](3821855.md) [People's Defense Units](36497032.md) (YPG). Other competing factions included [jihadist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jihadist) organizations such as the [Islamic State](9087364.md) (IS) and [al-Qaeda](1921.md)'s Syrian branch [Hurras al-Din](56718004.md) (the successor of [Al-Nusra Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Nusra_Front)).&#x20;

The civil war also served as a [proxy war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proxy_war)[^cite_note-14] as foreign countries–including [Iran](14653.md), [Russia](25391.md), [Turkey](11125639.md) and the [United States](3434750.md)–became [directly involved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_involvement_in_the_Syrian_civil_war) in the conflict, providing support to opposing factions. Iran, Russia and Lebanese [Hezbollah](13919.md) supported Assad's government militarily, with Iran [intervening in 2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_intervention_in_the_Syrian_civil_war) and Russia conducting [airstrikes and ground operations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_military_intervention_in_the_Syrian_civil_war) in the country beginning in September 2015. In 2014, the [US-led international coalition](44618350.md) officially began [conducting air and ground operations](43683196.md)–primarily against the Islamic State, al-Qaeda elements such as Hurras al-Din and the [Khorasan group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khorasan_group) and occasionally [against pro-Assad forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_attacks_on_Syria_during_the_Syrian_civil_war)–and militarily and logistically supporting factions such as the SDF and the [Syrian Free Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Commando_Army). [Turkish forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_involvement_in_the_Syrian_civil_war) [occupied parts of northern Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_occupation_of_northern_Syria) and fought the SDF, Assad government and Islamic State alike while actively supporting the SNA. Between 2011 and 2017, fighting from the Syrian civil war [spilled over](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_civil_war_spillover_in_Lebanon) into neighboring [Lebanon](17771.md) as opponents and supporters of the Syrian government traveled there to attack each other on Lebanese soil. While officially neutral, [Israel](9282173.md) [exchanged border fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli%E2%80%93Syrian_ceasefire_line_incidents_during_the_Syrian_civil_war) and [conducted repeated attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Israel_conflict_during_the_Syrian_civil_war) against Hezbollah and Iranian elements inside Syria, whose presence in the country it [viewed as a security threat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_and_the_Syrian_civil_war).[^cite_note-15][^cite_note-16]&#x20;

Violence in the war peaked during 2012–2017 amid rebel and government ground offensives alongside [sectarian](34766543.md) and Islamist violence.[^cite_note-17][^cite_note-18] By 2019 the Islamic State in Syria had been forced into an [insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Syria_insurgency) after years of territorial and personnel loss due to clashing with most other factions, including opposing jihadist groups, [Assad's forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Desert_campaign_\(2017%E2%80%932024\)) and allies, [Kurdish conquest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deir_ez-Zor_campaign_\(2017%E2%80%932019\)) and US-led operations. International organizations accused virtually all belligerents involved—the Assad government, the Islamic State, opposition groups, Iran, Russia,[^cite_note-The_Guardian-19] Turkey[^cite_note-amnesty1-20] and the US-led coalition[^cite_note-21]—of severe [human rights violations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_violations) and [massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_massacres_during_the_Syrian_civil_war).[^cite_note-22] The conflict caused [a major refugee crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugees_of_the_Syrian_civil_war), with millions of people fleeing to neighboring countries such as Turkey, Lebanon and [Jordan](7515964.md);[^cite_note-23][^cite_note-PBS-24] however, a sizable minority also sought refuge in countries outside of the Middle East, with [Germany](11867.md) alone accepting over half a million Syrians from 2011 to 2019.[^cite_note-PBS-24] [Numerous peace initiatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_peace_process) were launched since the start of the war, including several led by the [United Nations](31769.md), but fighting continued.[^cite_note-Mediation_in_Syria-25]&#x20;

After the [2020 Idlib ceasefire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwestern_Syria_offensive_\(2019%E2%80%932020\)#Second_Russia%E2%80%93Turkey_ceasefire), frontline clashes between Syrian government and rebel forces mostly subsided and the war effectively became a stalemate, although sporadic clashes and minor skirmishes between opposing factions continued. From 2021 to late 2024, Assad was consolidating power and slowly reversing the diplomatic isolation resulting from the war,[^cite_note-GuardianAssad2021-26][^cite_note-aje15march-2] however this was halted after [lightning offensives](78460726.md) by opposition groups across the country successfully collapsed his regime and caused him to flee to Russia. The civil war concluded with the creation of a [new provisional government](79584988.md) which integrated elements of both the SIG and SSG alternative governments. The transitional government, led by [Ahmed al-Sharaa](44007699.md), would go on to experience lower-level fighting within the country in the [aftermath of the war](81373641.md), while also overseeing reconstruction efforts and reinventing the country's diplomatic standing.&#x20;

See also: [Ba'athist Syria](57134800.md), [Presidency of Hafez al-Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Hafez_al-Assad), [Presidency of Bashar al-Assad](69538970.md), and [Assad family](27334130.md)

The [Arab Socialist Ba'ath Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba%27ath_Party) government came to power through [a coup d'état in 1963](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1963_Syrian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat) by overthrowing the [Second Syrian Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Syrian_Republic). A [second coup in 1966](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1966_Syrian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat) ousted the old Baathist leadership of [Michel Aflaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Aflaq), replacing it with a militaristic, hard-left, pro-Soviet regime led by [Salah Jadid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salah_Jadid), causing a split between the [Syrian branch of Ba'ath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Socialist_Ba%27ath_Party_%E2%80%93_Syria_Region), which supported Jadid, and the [Iraqi branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Socialist_Ba%27ath_Party_%E2%80%93_Iraq_Region), which remained loyal to Aflaq. Jadid was in turn [removed in November 1970](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corrective_Movement_\(Syria\)) by General [Hafez al-Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafez_al-Assad), an [Alawite](436616.md) who declared himself [President](516106.md) in March 1971.[^cite_note-27] This marked the beginning of the domination of personality cults centred around the [Assad family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Assad_family) that pervaded all aspects of Syrian daily life and was accompanied by a systematic suppression of civil and political freedoms, becoming the central feature of state propaganda. Authority in Ba'athist Syria was monopolised by three power-centres: Alawite loyalist clans, the Ba'ath Party and the [Syrian Armed Forces](27479.md). All three united by their allegiance to the Assad family.[^cite_note-28][^cite_note-29][^cite_note-Dictators_and_Autocrats-30]&#x20;

The Syrian Regional Branch remained the dominant political authority in what had been a [one-party state](102459.md) until the first [multi-party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-party_system) [election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Syrian_parliamentary_election) to the [People's Council of Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People%27s_Council_of_Syria) was held in 2012.[^cite_note-31] On 31 January 1973, Hafez al-Assad implemented a new constitution, leading to a national crisis. The [1973 Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Constitution_of_1973) entrusted the Arab Socialist Ba'ath Party with the distinctive role as the "leader of the state and society", empowering it to mobilise the civilians for party programmes, issue decrees to ascertain their loyalty and supervise all legal trade unions. Ba'athist ideology was imposed upon children as a compulsory part of school curricula as the Armed Forces became highly monitored by the Party. The constitution removed Islam from being recognised as the [state religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_religion) and stripped existing provisions such as the requirement that the president of Syria be [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim). These measures caused widespread furor amongst the public, leading to fierce demonstrations in Hama, Homs and Aleppo organized by the [Muslim Brotherhood](20742.md) and the *[ulama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulama)*. The Assad regime violently crushed the [Islamic revolts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamist_uprising_in_Syria) that occurred during 1976–1982, waged by revolutionaries from the [Syrian Muslim Brotherhood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Muslim_Brotherhood).[^cite_note-32]&#x20;

The Ba'ath Party carefully constructed Assad as the guiding father figure of the party and modern Syrian nation, advocating the continuation of [Assad dynastic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assad_dynasty) rule of Syria. As part of the publicity efforts to brand the nation and Assad family as inseparable, slogans such as "Assad or we burn the country", "Assad or to hell with the country" and "[Hafez Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafez_al-Assad), forever" became an integral part of the state and party discourse during the 1980s. Eventually the party organisation itself became a rubber stamp and the power structures became deeply dependent on sectarian affiliation to the Assad family and the central role of armed forces needed to crack down on dissent in the society. Critics of the regime have pointed out that deployment of violence is central to the rule of [Ba'athist Syria](57134800.md) and describe it as "a dictatorship with [genocidal](12441.md) tendencies".[^cite_note-Kmak,_Björklund_2022_73–74-33] Hafez al-Assad's nearly three-decade rule was marked by its methods, ranging from censorship to violent measures of [state terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_terror) such as [mass murders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_murders), [forced deportations](412990.md) and brutal practices such as [torture](47702.md), which were unleashed collectively upon the civilian population.[^cite_note-Dictators_and_Autocrats-30] Upon Hafez al-Assad's death in 2000, his son Bashar al-Assad succeeded him as the president of Syria.[^cite_note-Kmak,_Björklund_2022_73–74-33]&#x20;

Bashar's wife [Asma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asma_al-Assad), a [Sunni Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Muslim) born and educated in Britain, was initially hailed in the Western press as a "rose in the desert".[^cite_note-rose-34][^cite_note-35] The couple once raised hopes amongst Syrian intellectuals and outside Western observers, being seen as a path towards implementing economic and political reforms. However, Bashar failed to deliver on promised reforms, instead cracking down on the civil society groups, political reformists and democratic activists that emerged during the [Damascus Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damascus_Spring) in the 2000s.[^cite_note-nyt1Apr11-36] Bashar Al-Assad claims that no 'moderate opposition' to his government exists, and that all opposition forces are [Islamists](15012.md) focused on destroying his [secular leadership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularity); his view was that terrorist groups operating in Syria are "linked to the agendas of foreign countries".[^cite_note-37]&#x20;

### Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Syria)

The total Syrian population in July 2018 was estimated at 19,454,263 people. By ethnic groups, Syria was approximately [Arab](8862873.md) 50%, [Alawite](436616.md) 15%, [Kurd](3821855.md) 10%, [Levantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levantine_Arabic) 10% and 15% of other ethnic groups (includes [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze_people_in_Syria), [Ismaili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isma%27ilism), [Imami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imamate_\(Twelver_doctrine\)), [Assyrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_people), [Turkmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Turkmen) and [Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenians_in_Syria)). Its religious breakdown was: [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) 87% (official; includes [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam) 74% and Alawi, Ismaili and [Shia](26961.md) 13%), [Christian](18337522.md) 10% (mainly of [Eastern Christian churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Christianity)[^cite_note-IRFR2006-38]—may now be smaller as a result of Christians fleeing the country), [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze) 3% and [Jewish](25955086.md) (uncounted in the estimate, but with few remaining in Damascus and Aleppo).[^cite_note-39]&#x20;

### Socioeconomic background

[Socioeconomic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socioeconomics) inequality increased significantly after [free market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_market) policies were initiated by Hafez al-Assad in his later years, and it accelerated after Bashar al-Assad came to power. With an emphasis on the [service sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Service_sector), these policies benefited a minority of the nation's population, mostly people who had connections with the government, and members of the Sunni merchant class of Damascus and Aleppo.[^cite_note-Poor_rural_rebels-40] In 2010, Syria's nominal GDP per capita was only \$2,834, comparable to [sub-Saharan African](27067.md) countries such as [Nigeria](21383.md) and far lower than its neighbors such as Lebanon, with an annual growth rate of 3.39%, below most other developing countries.[^cite_note-41]&#x20;

The country also faced particularly high [youth unemployment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youth_unemployment) rates.[^cite_note-42] At the start of the war, discontent with the government was strongest in Syria's poor areas, predominantly among conservative Sunnis.[^cite_note-Poor_rural_rebels-40] These included cities with high poverty rates, such as [Daraa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daraa) and [Homs](77533.md), and the poorer districts of large cities.&#x20;

### Drought

The unrest coincided with the most intense drought ever recorded in Syria, which lasted from 2006 to 2011 and resulted in widespread crop failure, an increase in [food prices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_prices) and a mass migration of farming families to urban centers.[^cite_note-Kelley_drought_2015-43] This migration strained infrastructure already burdened by the influx of some 1.5 million refugees from the [Iraq War](5043324.md).[^cite_note-44] The drought has also been linked to [anthropogenic global warming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthropogenic_global_warming), with evidence that human-caused climate change influenced the severity and duration of the regional drought.[^cite_note-45][^cite_note-46][^cite_note-47] Some analysis, however, has challenged the relative contribution of climatic changes to the conflict, arguing that other factors played a more important role.[^cite_note-48][^cite_note-49][^cite_note-50] Adequate water supply continues to be an issue in the ongoing civil war and is frequently the target of military action.[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52]&#x20;

### Human rights

Main articles: [Human rights in Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Syria) and [Human rights violations during the Syrian civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_violations_during_the_Syrian_civil_war)

The human rights situation in Syria has long been the subject of harsh critique from global organizations.[^cite_note-53] The rights of [free expression](21401843.md), [association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_association) and [assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_assembly) were strictly controlled in Syria even before the uprising.[^cite_note-HRW-54] The country remained under a [state of emergency](298608.md) from 1963 until 2011 and public gatherings of more than five people were banned.[^cite_note-Reuters16Apr11-55] Security forces had sweeping powers of arrest and detention.[^cite_note-AmInt2009-56] Despite hopes for democratic change with the 2000 [Damascus Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damascus_Spring), Bashar al-Assad was widely reported as having failed to implement any improvements. In 2010, he imposed a controversial national ban on female [Islamic dress codes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_clothing) (such as [face veils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niq%C4%81b)) across universities, where reportedly over a thousand primary school teachers that wore the niqab were reassigned to administrative jobs.[^cite_note-57] A [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) report issued just before the beginning of the 2011 uprising stated that Assad had failed to substantially improve the state of human rights since taking power.[^cite_note-58]&#x20;

## History

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of the Syrian civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Syrian_civil_war).

### Protests, civil uprising, and armed insurgency (2011–2019)

Main articles: [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring) and [Syrian revolution](39750126.md)

For a chronological guide, see [January–April 2011](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Syrian_civil_war_\(January%E2%80%93April_2011\)), [May–August 2011](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Syrian_civil_war_\(May%E2%80%93August_2011\)), [September–December 2011](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Syrian_civil_war_\(September%E2%80%93December_2011\)) and [January–April 2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Syrian_civil_war_\(January%E2%80%93April_2012\)) timelines.

Further information: [Syrian revolution](39750126.md), [Early insurgency phase of the Syrian civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_insurgency_phase_of_the_Syrian_civil_war), and [Kofi Annan Syrian peace plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kofi_Annan_Syrian_peace_plan)

In March 2011, popular discontent with President [Bashar al-Assad](364813.md)'s [Ba'athist government](57134800.md) led to large-scale protests and [pro-democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro-democracy) rallies across Syria, as part of the wider [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring) protests in the region.[^cite_note-Kassam,_Becker_3-59][^cite_note-Syria:_The_story_of_the_conflict-60] Numerous protests were violently suppressed by security forces in [deadly crackdowns](39750126.md#Crackdown) ordered by Assad, resulting in tens of thousands of deaths and [detentions](13435705.md), many of whom were civilians.[^cite_note-Kassam,_Becker_3-59][^cite_note-Syria:_The_story_of_the_conflict-60] The [Syrian revolution](39750126.md) transformed into an [insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_insurgency_phase_of_the_Syrian_civil_war) with the formation of [resistance militias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Syrian_Army#2011_%E2%80%93_formation) across the country, developing into a full civil war by 2012.[^cite_note-61]&#x20;

Further information: [2019 Turkish offensive into north-eastern Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Turkish_offensive_into_north-eastern_Syria)

In October 2019, Kurdish leaders of the AANES announced they had reached a major deal with the Assad government, allowing for Syrian Army forces to enter Kurdish-held towns along the [Syria–Turkey border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria%E2%80%93Turkey_border). The deal was part of an effort to resist Turkey's [cross-border incursion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Turkish_offensive_into_north-eastern_Syria) into AANES territory after US forces [withdrew](43683196.md#Withdrawal_from_north_Syria) from the area after the collapse of the [Northern Syria Buffer Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Syria_Buffer_Zone).[^cite_note-62][^cite_note-63][^cite_note-64] In November 2019, Russia, Turkey and the Assad government established a [new buffer zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Northern_Syria_Buffer_Zone) in northern Syria that deescalated the Kurdish-Turkish clashes.[^cite_note-Turkey_and_Russia_Deal-65] US-led coalition forces regrouped in eastern Syria in continued support of the SDF against the [Islamic State insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Syria_insurgency), amid tensions with local Russian forces and Iranian elements in the region.[^cite_note-66][^cite_note-67]&#x20;

By the end of the decade, the war had resulted in an estimated 470,000–610,000 violent deaths, making it the second-deadliest conflict of the 21st century, after the [Second Congo War](877492.md).[^cite_note-68]&#x20;

### Stalemate and frozen conflict (2020–2024)

For a chronological guide, see [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Syrian_civil_war_\(2020\)), [2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Syrian_civil_war_\(2021\)), [2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Syrian_civil_war_\(2022\)), [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Syrian_civil_war_\(2023\)) and [January–October 2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Syrian_civil_war_\(January%E2%80%93October_2024\)) timelines.

Further information: [Northwestern Syria offensive (2019–2020)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwestern_Syria_offensive_\(2019%E2%80%932020\)), [Operation Spring Shield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Spring_Shield), and [Southern Syria protests (2023–24)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Syria_protests_\(2023%E2%80%9324\))

Following the March 2020 [Idlib ceasefire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwestern_Syria_offensive_\(2019%E2%80%932020\)#Second_Russia%E2%80%93Turkey_ceasefire), frontline fighting between the Syrian government under Assad and opposition groups had mostly subsided. By 2021, the Assad government controlled about two-thirds of the country and was consolidating power.[^cite_note-GuardianAssad2021-26][^cite_note-aje15march-2] Although, regular flare-ups occurred among factions in northwestern Syria, and [large-scale protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Syrian_protests) emerged in southern Syria and spread nationwide in response to extensive autocratic policies and the economic situation. The protests were noted at the time as resembling the 2011 revolution that preceded the civil war.[^cite_note-69][^cite_note-70][^cite_note-71][^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73]&#x20;

The civil war had largely settled into a stalemate by early 2023. The [United States Institute of Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Institute_of_Peace) said:&#x20;

> Twelve years into Syria's devastating civil war, the conflict appears to have settled into a frozen state. Although roughly 30% of the country is controlled by opposition forces, heavy fighting has largely ceased and there is a growing regional trend toward normalizing relations with the regime of Bashar al-Assad. Over the last decade, the conflict erupted into one of the most complicated in the world, with a dizzying array of international and regional powers, opposition groups, proxies, local militias and extremist groups all playing a role. The Syrian population has been brutalized, with nearly a half a million killed, 12 million fleeing their homes to find safety elsewhere, and widespread poverty and hunger. Meanwhile, efforts to broker a political settlement have gone nowhere, leaving the Assad regime firmly in power.[^cite_note-74]&#x20;

The US [Council on Foreign Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_on_Foreign_Relations) said:&#x20;

> &#x20;The war whose brutality once dominated headlines has settled into an uncomfortable stalemate. Hopes for regime change have largely died out, peace talks have been fruitless, and some regional governments are reconsidering their opposition to engaging with Syrian leader Bashar al-Assad. The government has regained control of most of the country, and Assad's hold on power seems secure.[^cite_note-cfr_2023-75]&#x20;

However, major clashes continued between Turkish forces and factions within Syria. In late 2023, Turkish forces continued to attack Kurdish forces in northern Syria.[^cite_note-76] Starting on 5 October 2023, the [Turkish Armed Forces](30205.md) launched a series of air and ground strikes targeting the Syrian Democratic Forces in northeastern Syria (AANES territory). The airstrikes were launched in response to the [2023 Ankara bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Ankara_bombing), which the Turkish government alleged was carried out by attackers originating from northeastern Syria.[^cite_note-reuters1-77]&#x20;

### Fall of the Assad regime (2024)



[Syrian opposition offensives](78460726.md) which overthrew Assad's regime in 11 days



Military situation following the [fall of the Assad regime](78548066.md) and the end of the civil war

See also: [Fall of the Assad regime](78548066.md), [Syrian caretaker government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_caretaker_government), [Syrian transitional government](79584988.md), [2024 Syrian opposition offensives](78460726.md), [Operation Dawn of Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Dawn_of_Freedom), and [Aftermath of the Syrian civil war](81373641.md)

For a chronological guide, see [November 2024–present](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Syrian_civil_war_\(November_2024%E2%80%93present\)) timeline.

On 27 November 2024, a coalition of opposition groups called the [Military Operations Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Operations_Command_\(Syria\)),[^cite_note-78] led by [Hay'at Tahrir al-Sham](53015755.md), launched [a major offensive](78460726.md)[^cite_note-79] against the Syrian Army and other pro-government forces in [Aleppo](7444618.md), [Idlib](7445043.md), [Hama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hama_Governorate) and [Homs Governorates](7445154.md).[^cite_note-80] This was followed by other rebel offensives from the [Southern Operations Room](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Operations_Room), the SDF and the Syrian Free Army which all began seizing Syrian government territory in the country's south and east.[^cite_note-81][^cite_note-82][^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84] On 29 November, rebel forces [entered Aleppo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Aleppo_\(2024\)) as Syrian Army positions collapsed across the country.[^cite_note-85] On 7 December, rebel forces [entered Damascus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Damascus_\(2024\)) and the next day, on 8 December, Bashar al-Assad was reported to have fled the capital.[^cite_note-86] The Syrian Army confirmed Assad was no longer in power and had fled the country,[^cite_note-Syrian_rebels_topple_Assad-87][^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89] resulting in the [collapse of his regime](78548066.md) and ending over 60 years of Ba'athist rule under the [Assad dynasty](27334130.md).[^cite_note-90][^cite_note-91][^cite_note-92][^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94] Assad and his family fled to [Moscow](19004.md) and were granted asylum in Russia.[^cite_note-Syrian_rebels_topple_Assad-87][^cite_note-95][^cite_note-96][^cite_note-97] The fall of Assad has been said to mark the end of the Syrian civil war.[^cite_note-end_of_war-4]&#x20;

Syrian prime minister [Mohammad Ghazi al-Jalali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Ghazi_al-Jalali) recognized the transfer of power to the [Syrian Salvation Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Salvation_Government), which established [a caretaker government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_caretaker_government) in Damascus with [Mohammed al-Bashir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_al-Bashir) serving as the prime minister.[^cite_note-98] [Ahmed al-Sharaa](44007699.md), the leader of the Syrian Salvation Government and [emir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emir) of Hay'at Tahrir al-Sham, became *de facto* leader of Syria.[^cite_note-99]&#x20;

## Belligerents

Main article: [Belligerents in the Syrian civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belligerents_in_the_Syrian_civil_war)



Local, regional and international actors involved in the Syrian civil war prior to the fall of the Assad regime.

### Syrian factions

There are numerous factions, both foreign and domestic, involved in the Syrian civil war. These can be divided into four main groups.&#x20;

- First, [Ba'athist Syria](57134800.md) led by [Bashar al-Assad](364813.md) and backed by his [Russian](25391.md) and [Iranian](14653.md) allies.

- Second, the [Syrian opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_opposition_\(2011%E2%80%932024\)) consisting of two alternative governments:&#x20;

  - i) the [Syrian Interim Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Interim_Government) backed by the [Syrian National Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Coalition_of_Syrian_Revolutionary_and_Opposition_Forces), a [big-tent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_tent) coalition of [democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy), [Syrian nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_nationalism) and [Islamic political](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_aspects_of_Islam) groups whose defense forces consist of the [Syrian National Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_National_Army)

    [^cite_note-100]

    &#x20;and [Free Syrian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Syrian_Army), and

  - ii) the [Syrian Salvation Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Salvation_Government), a [Sunni Islamist](15012.md) coalition led by [Hay'at Tahrir al-Sham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahrir_al-Sham).

    [^cite_note-101]

- Third, the Kurdish-dominated [Autonomous Administration of North and East Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Administration_of_North_and_East_Syria) and its military wing, the [Syrian Democratic Forces](48205248.md), supported by the [United States](3434750.md), [France](5843419.md) and other [coalition allies](44618350.md).

  [^cite_note-102]

- Fourth, the [global jihadist](7826589.md) camp consisting of [al-Qaeda](1921.md) affiliate [Guardians of Religion Organisation](56718004.md) and its rival [Islamic State](9087364.md).

  [^cite_note-103]

The Syrian government, the opposition and the SDF all received support—militarily, logistically and diplomatically—from foreign countries, leading the conflict to often be described as a [proxy war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proxy_war).[^cite_note-104]&#x20;

### Foreign involvement

Main article: [Foreign involvement in the Syrian civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_involvement_in_the_Syrian_civil_war)

See also: [Israel and the Syrian civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_and_the_Syrian_civil_war)



Map of states with military/paramilitary forces deployed in Syria.

&#x20; Syria

&#x20; Supported the Assad government with troops

&#x20; Supported the Assad government with militia

&#x20; Supporting Syrian rebels or a non-government faction with troops

The major parties that supported the Assad government were [Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_intervention_in_the_Syrian_civil_war),[^cite_note-105] [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_involvement_in_the_Syrian_civil_war)[^cite_note-telegraph-106] and Lebanese [militia](20622.md) [Hezbollah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hezbollah_involvement_in_the_Syrian_civil_war). Syrian rebel groups received political, logistic and military support from the [United States](15996874.md),[^cite_note-107][^cite_note-108] [Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_involvement_in_the_Syrian_civil_war),[^cite_note-109] [Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabian_involvement_in_the_Syrian_civil_war),[^cite_note-110] [Qatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qatari_involvement_in_the_Syrian_civil_war),[^cite_note-111] Britain, France,[^cite_note-112] [Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel%27s_role_in_the_Syrian_civil_war)[^cite_note-113][^cite_note-114] and the [Netherlands](21148.md).[^cite_note-115] Under the aegis of operation [Timber Sycamore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timber_Sycamore) and other clandestine activities, [CIA operatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Activities_Center) and [US special operations troops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_special_operations_forces) have trained and armed nearly 10,000 rebel fighters at a cost of \$1 billion a year since 2012.[^cite_note-116][^cite_note-117] [Iraq](7515928.md) had also been involved in supporting the Syrian government, but mostly against [ISIL](9087364.md).[^cite_note-118]&#x20;

[Hezbollah](13919.md), the Lebanese Shia militant group, was significantly involved in the Syrian Civil War. Starting from the 2011 Syrian revolution, Hezbollah provided active support to the Ba'athist government forces.[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120] By 2012, the group escalated its involvement, deploying troops across Syria.[^cite_note-121] In 2013, Hezbollah publicly acknowledged its presence in Syria, intensifying its ground commitment. This involvement included an estimated 5,000 to 8,000 fighters at any given time, comprising [Special Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_forces), standing forces from all units, part-time fighters and new recruits with accelerated combat training. Hezbollah's presence, supported by Iranian weaponry and training, further complicated the conflict dynamics, drawing Israeli airstrikes against Hezbollah and Iranian targets in Syria.[^cite_note-122]&#x20;

### Spillover

Main article: [Spillover of the Syrian civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spillover_of_the_Syrian_civil_war)

Further information: [War in Iraq (2013–2017)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Iraq_\(2013%E2%80%932017\))

In June 2014, members of the [Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant](9087364.md) (ISIL) crossed the border from Syria into northern Iraq, and [took control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Iraq_offensive_\(June_2014\)) of large swaths of Iraqi territory as the [Iraqi Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Army) abandoned its positions. Fighting between rebels and government forces also spilled over into Lebanon on several occasions. There were repeated incidents of sectarian violence in the [North Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Governorate) of Lebanon between supporters and opponents of the Syrian government, as well as armed clashes between Sunnis and Alawites in [Tripoli](268796.md).[^cite_note-123]&#x20;

Starting on 5 June 2014, ISIL seized swathes of territory in Iraq. As of 2014, the [Syrian Arab Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Arab_Air_Force) used airstrikes targeted against ISIL in [Raqqa](858965.md) and [al-Hasakah](7573335.md) in coordination with the Iraqi government.[^cite_note-124]&#x20;

## Weaponry and warfare

See also: [List of equipment of the Syrian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_equipment_of_the_Syrian_Army), [List of military equipment used by Syrian opposition forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_military_equipment_used_by_Syrian_opposition_forces), and [Improvised artillery in the Syrian civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Improvised_artillery_in_the_Syrian_civil_war)



'Hell cannon' [Improvised artillery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Improvised_artillery_in_the_Syrian_civil_war) found after the [battle of Aleppo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Aleppo_\(2012%E2%80%932016\)) in 2016



Syrian Army [T-72](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-72) tank during the [2018 Rif Dimashq offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rif_Dimashq_offensive_\(February%E2%80%93April_2018\))

### Chemical weapons

Main articles: [Use of chemical weapons in the Syrian civil war](39668667.md) and [Destruction of Syria's chemical weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Destruction_of_Syria%27s_chemical_weapons)

See also: [Syria and weapons of mass destruction](13895017.md) and [Syria chemical weapons program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria_chemical_weapons_program)

[Sarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarin), [mustard agent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustard_agent) and [chlorine gas](5667.md#Use_as_a_weapon) have been used during the conflict. Numerous casualties led to an international reaction, especially the 2013 [Ghouta chemical attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghouta_chemical_attack). The attack was the deadliest use of chemical weapons since the [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War#Iraq's_use_of_chemical_weapons).[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126] [A UN fact-finding mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Mission_to_Investigate_Alleged_Uses_of_Chemical_Weapons_in_the_Syrian_Arab_Republic) was requested to investigate reported chemical weapons attacks. In four cases, UN inspectors confirmed the use of [sarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarin) gas.[^cite_note-127] In August 2016, a confidential report by the UN and the [OPCW](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OPCW) explicitly blamed the Syrian military of Bashar al-Assad for dropping chemical weapons (chlorine bombs) on the towns of Talmenes in April 2014 and Sarmin in March 2015 and ISIL for using sulfur mustard on the town of Marea in August 2015.[^cite_note-128]&#x20;

The United States and the European Union have said the Syrian government has conducted several chemical attacks. Following the 2013 Ghouta attacks and international pressure, the [destruction of Syria's chemical weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Destruction_of_Syria%27s_chemical_weapons) began. In 2015 the UN mission disclosed previously undeclared traces of sarin compounds at a "military research site".[^cite_note-129] After the April 2017 [Khan Shaykhun chemical attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khan_Shaykhun_chemical_attack), the United States launched its first intentional attack against Syrian government forces. An investigation conducted by Tobias Schneider and Theresa Lutkefend of the [GPPi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Public_Policy_Institute) research institute documented 336 confirmed attacks involving chemical weapons in Syria between 23 December 2012 and 18 January 2019. The study attributed 98% of the total chemical attacks to the Assad regime. Almost 90% of the attacks occurred after Ghouta chemical attack in August 2013.[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-131]&#x20;

In April 2020, the UN Security Council briefing was held on the findings of a global chemical weapons watchdog, [Organisation for the Prohibition of Chemical Weapons](36832.md) (OPCW), which found that the [Syrian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Air_Force) used sarin and chlorine during multiple attacks in 2017. Syria's close allies, Russia, and other European countries debated the issue, during which Moscow dismissed the OPCW findings while many Western European countries called for accountability for the government's [war crimes](48626.md).[^cite_note-132] The UN Deputy ambassador from Britain, Jonathan Allen, stated that the report by the OPCW's Investigation Identification Team (IIT) claimed that the Syrian regime is responsible for using [chemical weapons](27179600.md) in the war on at least four occasions. The information was also noted in two UN-mandated investigations.[^cite_note-133]&#x20;

In April 2021, Syria was suspended from the OPCW through the public vote of member states for not cooperating with the IIT and for violating the [Chemical Weapons Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_Weapons_Convention).[^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135][^cite_note-136] Findings of another OPCW investigation report published in July 2021 concluded that the Syrian regime had engaged in confirmed chemical attacks at least 17 times, out of the 77 reported incidents of chemical weapons usage attributed to [Assadist](36629941.md) forces.[^cite_note-137][^cite_note-138]&#x20;

### Cluster bombs

Syria is not a party to the [Convention on Cluster Munitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_on_Cluster_Munitions) and does not recognize the ban on the use of [cluster bombs](79745.md). The Syrian Army is reported to have begun using cluster bombs in September 2012. Steve Goose, director of the Arms Division at [Human Rights Watch](46530.md), said "Syria is expanding its relentless use of cluster munitions, a banned weapon, and civilians are paying the price with their lives and limbs." He adds of the weapons that "The initial toll is only the beginning because cluster munitions often leave unexploded bomblets that kill and maim long afterward."[^cite_note-139]&#x20;

### Thermobaric weapons

Russian [thermobaric weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermobaric_weapon), also known as "fuel-air bombs", were used by the government's side during the war. On 2 December 2015, *[The National Interest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_National_Interest)* reported that Russia was deploying the [TOS-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TOS-1) Buratino multiple rocket launch system to Syria, which is "designed to launch massive thermobaric charges against infantry in confined spaces such as urban areas".[^cite_note-Russia's_Lethal_Thermobaric_Rocket_Launchers-140] One [Buratino thermobaric rocket launcher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TOS-1) "can obliterate a roughly 200 by 400 metres (660 by 1,310 feet) area with a single salvo".[^cite_note-Russia's_Lethal_Thermobaric_Rocket_Launchers-140] Since 2012, rebels have said that the Syrian Air Force (government forces) is using thermobaric weapons against residential areas occupied by the rebel fighters, such as during the [Battle of Aleppo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Aleppo_\(2012%E2%80%932016\)) and also in [Kafr Batna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafr_Batna).[^cite_note-141] A panel of United Nations human rights investigators reported that the Syrian government used thermobaric bombs against the strategic town of Qusayr in March 2013.[^cite_note-142] In August 2013, the BBC reported on the use of napalm-like incendiary bombs on a school in northern Syria.[^cite_note-143]&#x20;

### Missiles

See also: [Operation Laylat al-Qadr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Laylat_al-Qadr)



An [Army of Glory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaysh_al-Izza) fighter launches a [BGM-71 TOW](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BGM-71_TOW) anti-tank missile at a Syrian government position during the [2017 Hama offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Hama_offensive).

Several types of [anti-tank missiles](37517.md) are in use in Syria. Russia has sent [9M133 Kornet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9M133_Kornet), third-generation anti-tank guided missiles to the Syrian government whose forces have used them extensively against armour and other ground targets to fight jihadists and rebels.[^cite_note-144] US-made [BGM-71 TOW](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BGM-71_TOW) missiles are one of the primary weapons of rebel groups and have been primarily provided by the United States and Saudi Arabia.[^cite_note-145] The US has also supplied many Eastern European sourced [9K111 Fagot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9K111_Fagot) launchers and warheads to Syrian rebel groups under its [Timber Sycamore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timber_Sycamore) program.[^cite_note-janes-20160408-146]&#x20;

In June 2017, Iran attacked ISIL targets in the [Deir ez-Zor](1429146.md) area in eastern Syria with [Zolfaghar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zolfaghar_\(missile\)) [ballistic missiles](38025.md) fired from western Iran,[^cite_note-janes.com-147] in the first use of mid-range missiles by Iran in 30 years.[^cite_note-148] According to *[Jane's Defence Weekly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jane%27s_Defence_Weekly)*, the missiles travelled 650–700 kilometres.[^cite_note-janes.com-147]&#x20;

## Sectarianism

Main articles: [Sectarianism and minorities in the Syrian civil war](34766543.md) and [Federalization of Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalization_of_Syria)



Map of Syria's ethno-religious composition in 1976

The successive governments of Hafez and Bashar al-Assad have been closely associated with the country's minority Alawite religious group, an offshoot of Shia, whereas the majority of the population, and most of the opposition, is Sunni. This resulted in calls for persecution of the Alawites by parts of the opposition.[^cite_note-Behari-149]&#x20;

A third of 250,000 Alawite men of military age have been killed fighting in the Syrian civil war.[^cite_note-150] In May 2013, [SOHR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SOHR) stated that out of 94,000 killed during the war, 41,000 of which being Alawites.[^cite_note-151]&#x20;

As militias and non-Syrian Shia—motivated by pro-Shia sentiment rather than loyalty to the Assad government—have taken over fighting the anti-government forces from the weakened Syrian Army, fighting has taken on a more sectarian nature. One opposition leader has said that the Shia militias often "try to occupy and control the religious symbols in the Sunni community to achieve not just a territorial victory but a sectarian one as well"—reportedly occupying mosques and replacing Sunni icons with pictures of Shia leaders. According to the Syrian Network for Human Rights, human rights abuses have been committed by the militias including "a series of sectarian massacres between March 2011 and January 2014 that left 962 civilians dead".[^cite_note-nelson-defector-152]&#x20;

In 2025 after the defeat of Assad and the rise of the transitional government, a series of [mass killings and massacres against Alawites](79404646.md) occurred in Syria, largely in March 2025. The UK-based Syrian Observatory for Human Rights (SOHR) reported that 1,614 civilians were killed by armed militias supporting the Syrian government. The observatory estimated actual numbers to be much higher.[^cite_note-153]&#x20;

## Kurdish autonomy in northeastern Syria

Main articles: [Autonomous Administration of North and East Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Administration_of_North_and_East_Syria) and [Rojava conflict](48303468.md)

The [Autonomous Administration of North and East Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Administration_of_North_and_East_Syria) (AANES), also known as Rojava,[^cite_note-157] is a [de facto](43594.md) [autonomous region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_administrative_division) in northeastern Syria.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllsoppvan_Wilgenburg201911,_95-158][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZabad2017219,_228-159] The region does not claim to pursue full independence but autonomy within a federal and democratic Syria.[^cite_note-MiddleEastEye-160] Rojava consists of self-governing [sub-regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_North_and_East_Syria) in the areas of [Afrin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrin_Region), [Jazira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazira_Region), [Euphrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euphrates_Region), [Raqqa](858965.md), [Tabqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Thawrah), [Manbij](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manbij) and [Deir Ez-Zor](7445749.md).[^cite_note-Second_conference_of_Shahba_region-161][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllsoppvan_Wilgenburg201997–98-162] The region gained its de facto autonomy in 2012 in the context of the ongoing [Rojava conflict](48303468.md), in which its official military force, the Syrian Democratic Forces (SDF), has taken part.[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164]&#x20;

While entertaining [some foreign relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Rojava), the region is not officially recognized as autonomous by the government of Syria or any state except for the [Catalan Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Catalonia).[^cite_note-165][^cite_note-166] The AANES has widespread support for its universal [democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy), [sustainable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological), [autonomous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous) [pluralist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pluralism_\(political_philosophy\)), [equal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equality_before_the_law) and [feminist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jineology) policies in dialogues with other political parties and organizations.[^cite_note-tandfonline.com-167][^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169][^cite_note-170] Northeastern Syria is [polyethnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polyethnic) and home to sizeable ethnic Kurdish, Arab and Assyrian populations, with smaller communities of ethnic Turkmen, Armenians, [Circassians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassians) and [Yazidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazidis).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllsoppvan_Wilgenburg2019xviii,_112-171][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZabad2017219,_228–229-172][^cite_note-173]&#x20;

The supporters of the region's administration state that it is an officially [secular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism) [polity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polity)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllsoppvan_Wilgenburg2019xviii,_66,_200-174][^cite_note-marriage-175] with [direct democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_democracy) ambitions based on an [anarchistic](12.md), feminist and [libertarian socialist ideology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_confederalism) promoting [decentralization](49139.md), [gender equality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_equality),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZabad2017219-176][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllsoppvan_Wilgenburg2019156–163-177] environmental [sustainability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainability), social [ecology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecology) and pluralistic tolerance for religious, cultural and [political diversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diversity_\(politics\)), and that these values are mirrored in [its constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_North_and_East_Syria), society and politics, stating it to be a model for a [federalized Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalization_of_Syria) as a whole, rather than outright independence.[^cite_note-MiddleEastEye-160][^cite_note-178][^cite_note-utopia-179][^cite_note-180][^cite_note-Jongerden-181] The region's administration has also been accused by some [partisan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partisan_\(politics\)) and nonpartisan sources of [authoritarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism), support of the Syrian government,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllsoppvan_Wilgenburg201994,_130–131,_184-182] [Kurdification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdification) and displacement.[^cite_note-\:4-183] However, despite this the AANES has been the most [democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_democracy) system in Syria, with direct open elections, [universal equality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_equality), respecting [human rights](13831.md) within the region, as well as defense of [minority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_group) and [religious rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_rights) within Syria.[^cite_note-tandfonline.com-167][^cite_note-184][^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187][^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189]&#x20;

In March 2015, the Syrian Information Minister announced that his government considered recognizing Kurdish autonomy "within the law and constitution".[^cite_note-190] While the region's administration was not invited to the [Geneva III peace talks on Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_peace_talks_on_Syria_\(2016\)),[^cite_note-191] or any of the earlier talks, Russia in particular called for the region's inclusion and did to some degree carry the region's positions into the talks, as documented in Russia's May 2016 draft for a new constitution for Syria.[^cite_note-Now\.MMedia/Al-Akhbar-192][^cite_note-Al-Monitor-193]&#x20;



The Kurdish-led [Syrian Democratic Forces](48205248.md) announcing the [Deir ez-Zor campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deir_ez-Zor_campaign_\(2017%E2%80%932019\)) in 2017

An analysis released in June 2017 described the region's "relationship with the government fraught but functional" and a "semi-cooperative dynamic".[^cite_note-194] In late September 2017, Syria's foreign minister said that Damascus would consider granting Kurds more autonomy in the region once ISIL was defeated.[^cite_note-195]&#x20;

On 13 October 2019, the SDF announced that it had reached an agreement with the Syrian Army which allowed the latter to enter the SDF-held cities of Manbij and Kobani in order to dissuade a Turkish attack on those cities as part of the cross-border offensive by Turkish and Turkish-backed Syrian rebels.[^cite_note-196] The Syrian Army also deployed in the north of Syria together with the SDF along the Syrian-Turkish border and entered into several SDF-held cities such as Ayn Issa and Tell Tamer.[^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198] Following the creation of the [Second Northern Syria Buffer Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Northern_Syria_Buffer_Zone), the SDF stated that it was ready to work cooperatively with the Syrian Army if a political settlement between the Syrian government and the SDF was achieved.[^cite_note-199]&#x20;

As of 2022, the main military threat and conflict faced by Rojava's official defense force, the Syrian Democratic Forces (SDF), are firstly, an ongoing conflict with ISIS; and secondly, ongoing concerns of possible invasion of the northeast regions of Syria by Turkish forces, in order to strike Kurdish groups in general, and Rojava in particular.[^cite_note-200][^cite_note-201][^cite_note-202] An official report by the Rojava government noted Turkey-backed militias as the main threat to the region of Rojava and its government.[^cite_note-203]&#x20;

On 5 June 2022, the leader of the SDF, [Mazloum Abdi](59386296.md), said that forces of the Kurdish government in the AANES were willing to work with Syrian government forces to defend against Turkey, saying "Damascus should use its air defense systems against Turkish planes." Abdi said that Kurdish groups would be able to cooperate with the Syrian government, and still retain their autonomy.[^cite_note-204][^cite_note-205][^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207][^cite_note-208] The joint discussions were a result of the negotiation processes that had begun in October 2019.[^cite_note-209] In early 2023, reports indicated that the forces of Islamic State in Syria had mostly been defeated, with only a few cells remaining in various remote locations.[^cite_note-210][^cite_note-211][^cite_note-212]&#x20;

As of 2023, Turkey was continuing its support for various militias within Syria, consisting mostly of the Syrian National Army, which periodically attempted some operations against Kurdish groups.[^cite_note-auto2-213][^cite_note-auto1-214][^cite_note-215] One stated goal was to create "safe zones" along Turkey's border with Syria, according to a statement by Turkish president Erdoğan.[^cite_note-auto3-216] The operations were generally aimed at the [Tal Rifaat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tal_Rifaat) and [Manbij](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manbij) regions west of the [Euphrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euphrates) and other areas further east. President Erdoğan openly stated his support for the operations, in talks with Moscow in mid-2022.[^cite_note-auto5-217]&#x20;
